NorthernKnife · creative research · September 2026 · for strategists, editors and designers

What makes a NorthernKnife video ad win

We took every video brief ever written in Notion (642 of them), named each one with the new v2.2 naming convention so that every creative decision becomes a tag (angle, hook tactic, structure, tone, proof, offer timing, avatar, product, voice, face, length…), and joined the 405 that ran on Meta to their real results in Triple Whale. This document is what the numbers say. It is the reference for the next brief you write: which angles and hooks start customer journeys, which ones only close people who already know us, and which ones we should stop making.

642briefs named
405ads launched on Meta
$1.12Mspend analysed
179readable ads ($1k+)
27winners (FC 2.0+)
93losers (FC under 1.0)

01The recipe, in one page

If you read nothing else: the best NorthernKnife ad is a craftsmanship or "return" story, opened with a question or a story line, walked through as an explainer, aimed at JOHN (the collector), on the Feather, with nobody on camera, a proof device (the forge, the guarantee, the customer count), the offer landing mid-script, and 90 to 130 seconds long. Ads built like that are 3 times more common among winners than among losers. Ads built the opposite way (a bold claim about a deal, urgent tone, a creator talking to camera, under 45 seconds, to the product page) are the bulk of our losers even when Ads Manager says they are fine.

Do this

  1. Lead with the making, not the deal. The forge, the three months, the blacksmith. Craftsmanship is the strongest single signal in the account.
  2. Open with a question or a story line. "What if a knife could be more than a knife." "This lunatic shipped back his Feather yesterday."
  3. Explain, or tell one story. Explainer and Story are the only structures that win more than they lose.
  4. Write for JOHN first. The collector buys on first touch; he is 37% of winners and 16% of losers.
  5. Put a proof device in the body. Maker, guarantee or customer count. Ads with no proof lose 67% of the time.
  6. Let the offer resolve the story, mid-script. Offer at 60 to 80% is the best timing; never in the hook.
  7. Go long. 90 to 130 seconds and 320+ words. Long ads start journeys, short ads collect view-through credit.
  8. Iterate winners the right way. Same script on another product, another editor or another market works. Re-hooking or re-cutting the body of a winner does not.

Stop doing this

  • Testimonial ads. 58 made, 4% won. Reviews read by a voice do not start a journey.
  • Bold-claim hooks by default. Our most used tactic (68 ads) and the one that loses most (68%).
  • Deal-first scripts. Value Offer angle, Offer-First structure, "deal alert", "ends tonight". Great Platform ROAS, 0.5 to 1.2 on First Click. Retargeting only.
  • Apology and confession openers. "We're sorry", "I lied": zero winners.
  • Contrarian, warning and joke hooks. "Don't buy a NorthernKnife" (FC 0.21), "Only hours left" (0.76), the Sydney Sweeney joke (0.42).
  • Creators talking to camera. UGC is the weakest type (FC 0.80, 80% loss); female creator voice never won.
  • Sending cold traffic to the PDP. FC 0.93 against 1.85 for the 6 Reasons listicle.
  • STEVE. 28 ads, zero winners. And be careful with MIKE and LOKI Blackout: hit or miss.

02How we measure a winner

Every ad is read under three attribution models. Platform is what Meta claims. Triple Attribution is Triple Whale's blended model. First Click credits the ad that started the customer's journey. First Click is the one that decides scale or kill at NorthernKnife, because it is the only one that tells us whether an ad brings in new people rather than closing people who already knew us. An ad with high Platform and low First Click is a retargeting asset, not a growth asset. This document shows all three every time, and reads First Click.

An ad is readable once it has spent $1,000; below that the ROAS is noise. Among readable ads, a winner is First Click ROAS 2.0 or more, a loser is under 1.0. Every table also shows the median First Click and the win and loss rates, because spend-weighted averages are pulled by a few huge ads: a trait is real when the average, the median and the win rate agree.

TierAdsSpendShare of spendPlatformTripleFirst ClickHook rateHold rateCTR
Winners (FC 2.0+)27$502,11945%1.871.962.6041.2%28.3%1.60%
Middle (1.0 to 2.0)59$286,24326%1.931.801.4432.8%23.4%1.22%
Losers (under 1.0)93$246,94322%1.921.610.6124.8%21.1%1.04%
Whole account405$1,120,336100%1.871.791.7234.3%24.9%1.33%

Hook rate = 3-second views / impressions. Hold rate = thruplays / 3-second views. CTR = link clicks / impressions.

Notice that Platform ROAS is the same for all three tiers (about 1.9). Meta cannot tell our winners from our losers. First Click can, and so can the audience: winners are watched past 3 seconds 41% of the time against 25% for losers, held 28% against 21%, and clicked 1.6% against 1.0%. A winner is visible in the first three seconds.

04Anatomy of the two biggest winners

NK184 · What if · CTA · Feather · $104,855 · Platform 1.60 · Triple 1.95 · First Click 2.87

The biggest ad in the account, and the clearest case of Platform under-reading a winner. Everything in it is the recipe. Angle is craftsmanship: "Hand-forged by a real blacksmith. One blade at a time... no two are ever exactly the same." Hook is a question, repeated as a refrain: "What if a knife could be more than a knife." Structure is an explainer built as a manifesto; the brief says "edit like some Nike commercial." Tone serious, proof the maker, nobody on camera, AI male voice. The product is revealed a third of the way in, and the offer lands at block 5 of 6, roughly 57% of the words, after the story has done its work. 106 seconds, 370 words. Its editor clone (NK326, $85k, FC 2.45) and its market clone (NK146, FC 2.71) won too: the script travels.

NK222 · Return Feather Lunatic · $87,000 · Platform 1.95 · Triple 2.06 · First Click 2.51

The other family. Angle is Return: a customer sends the knife back and the story of why is the ad. Hook is a story line: "Alright, this absolute lunatic shipped back his Feather Knife yesterday. And I've got to tell you why because you're not going to believe it." Structure is a story, tone funny, proof the guarantee, nobody on camera, 157 seconds. The offer appears early here (block 2 of 13), but it is part of the plot (he returned it because the deal launched four days after he ordered), not a deal announcement. Return Box (NK168, FC 2.75), Big Brain Return (NK284, 2.06), Return Box iteration 3 (NK219, 2.41) and Reopened Return (NK234, 2.43) all won. The only Return ads that lost changed the plot into an announcement (Customer Return NK212, FC 0.76: "not because it was bad, because our timing was").

What both share: a real story or a real reason to believe, a first line that opens a loop the body closes, no person selling to camera, and enough length to earn the offer.

05Angle: what the ad is about

Craftsmanship is the strongest signal in the account (FC 2.47, 43% win rate, three times more common among winners than losers). Return is the second family (2.04). Gifting holds up in the holiday databases (1.80, median 1.41). Value Offer is the most used angle after Return and a trap: Platform 2.37, First Click 1.19, 58% loss. Objection, Social Proof, Product Proof and Collection (which knife to pick) lose on every model that matters. Scarcity is a coin flip: Feather Back In Stock is the best FC in the account (3.80) and Collector's Last Piece one of the worst (0.14); it works when there is real news, not when it is a framing.

06Hook tactic: how the first line opens

Question (FC 2.32, as many wins as losses) and Story (1.85, the Return family) are the tactics to default to. Number works when the number is about the product ("5 reasons the Feather broke the internet") and fails when it is a gimmick ("3 reasons women are stealing this knife", 0.50). Roast ("your knife drawer is a graveyard of bad decisions") and Doubt ("is the Blackout worth the upgrade or just a paint job") win small but consistently. Bold Claim is our most used hook and loses 68% of the time; it only wins when the claim is news (Feather Back In Stock). Confession, Contrarian, Warning, Shock and Joke should not be written again for prospecting.

07Structure: how the body is built

Explainer (FC 2.35) is the only structure with more wins than losses; Story (1.96) is close behind. Listicle works in the holiday databases (3 Reasons Short, FC 3.58) and fails as a product-feature list (Functional Listicle, 0.42). Founder and News win when there is a real story or real news, and are too rare to lean on. Dialogue is a coin flip. Offer-First (2.18 Platform, 1.08 First Click) and Apology (2.20 / 1.11, zero winners) are retargeting structures. Testimonial is the structure to retire: 58 ads, 4% win, 58% loss. Reaction (replying to a comment) loses too.

08Tone

Serious (FC 2.18) wins because the craftsmanship ads are serious; Warm (1.72) carries the gifting winners; Blunt and Funny are average. Urgent is the trap: Platform 2.23, First Click 1.24, 71% loss. Urgency closes people who already wanted the knife; it does not make strangers want one.

09Proof element

Every winner but two carries a proof device. Maker (the blacksmith, the forge, the months) has the best average (2.04). Count (100,000 customers) has the best median (1.23). Guarantee is the safe default (1.78). Reviews as the proof is average and reviews as the whole ad (Testimonial) is a loser. No proof at all: 61 ads, FC 1.08, 67% loss. If the body does not answer "why should I believe you", the ad loses.

10The offer: when, how, which

The house rule holds in the data: the offer resolves the story, it never opens it. Offer mid-script (60 to 80% through) has the best First Click (1.98). Offer-first has the best Platform ROAS (2.10) and the weakest First Click (1.58). Naming the offer in the hook drops First Click from 1.75 to 1.18 with 62% loss. Which offer matters less than where it sits: B2G2 (1.82) beats B2G1 (1.52) beats no offer (1.20), but an offer-first B2G2 still loses.

11Avatar: who the ad speaks to

JOHN the collector is the First Click avatar: FC 2.11, 26% win, 37% of all winners against 16% of losers. SARAH (the gift buyer) holds up in holiday campaigns (1.70, best median at 1.32). MIKE the BBQ king is our most used avatar and hit or miss: 26% of winners, 43% of losers, FC 1.66 with 61% loss; he wins on Return stories and loses on deals and comparisons. VANCE the deal seeker is a retargeting avatar (Platform 2.13, First Click 1.06). STEVE: 28 ads, zero winners, retire him.

Creators are not a prospecting format for us today; they are a retargeting and social-proof format.

14Length and script length

Long wins. 90 to 130 seconds is the best band (FC 2.19), 130 seconds and more is close (2.03), 45 to 90 seconds is average (1.45), under 45 seconds is a trap (Platform 2.11, First Click 1.31, 53% loss). Script length says the same: 320+ words FC 2.21, under 120 words 1.14. Short ads collect view-through credit from people who already know us; long ads have room for the story, the proof and the offer in the right order. The caveat: the long band is where the NK184 and NK222 families live, so the median (0.95) is ordinary. Length does not make an ad win; it gives a good script the room to.

15New, iteration or adaptation

Iterations of winners beat new concepts: Ite FC 2.18 (median 1.32) against New 1.28. But only some iterations. Moving a winning script to another product (2.02), another editor (2.22) or another market (2.33) keeps what made it win. Changing the hook (0.98) or the body (0.90) of a winner loses it. Adaptations from other brands are rare and mixed: the Coolina swipes averaged 2.45 with 60% loss (one big win), Smooche and Ryze swipes lost.

The patterns behind them

  • Deal as the subject. Triple Discount (Platform 2.95 / FC 0.48), Deal Alert (2.50 / 0.59), Blackout Deal, Your Lucky Day, The Free Two Are The Good Ones. Each one is a Value Offer angle in an Offer-First structure. They convert warm traffic and start nothing.
  • Apology and confession. Blackout Sorry (0.54), I Lied (0.95), Owe You An Apology (1.60 at best). Six apology ads, zero winners.
  • Answering the objection out loud. Somebody Asked Us To Drop The Price (0.62, our biggest loser at $20k), Fake Amazon (0.85). Repeating the doubt to a stranger plants the doubt.
  • Which one to pick. Collection ads (0.64, 100% loss) and Collector's Last Piece (0.32, 0.14). People who do not know us yet do not want to choose between knives.
  • Short and claim-led. Most losers are under 50 seconds and open with a bold claim. Nothing is earned before the ask.

19Traps: good in Ads Manager, bad for growth

These traits have a Platform ROAS above 2.0 and a First Click ROAS near or below 1.2. They are not bad ads; they are retargeting ads. Keep them in retargeting ad sets and judge them on Platform there. Never scale them as prospecting and never let them set the template for the next brief.

20The brief checklist

Run every new prospecting brief through this before it goes to an editor. A brief that fails three or more lines is a retargeting brief, and should be labelled Fun-BOF and funded that way.

Subject and angle

  • The ad is about the knife being made, kept, or returned, not about the deal.
  • One angle, and it is Craftsmanship, Return, Gifting or a real piece of news.
  • No objection, no "which one", no "people are saying".

Hook

  • Hook 1 is a question or a story line, 2 to 3 sentences, setup then turn.
  • No offer, no price, no discount word in any hook.
  • No apology, no warning, no "don't buy", no joke as the opener.

Body

  • Explainer or Story structure; 320+ words for a 90 to 130 second cut.
  • One proof device named: the maker, the guarantee, the customer count.
  • The offer lands at 60 to 80% and is followed by 2 to 4 more beats.
  • No spec-sheet words (alloy codes, HRC, mm, grams).

Format and destination

  • AI male voice over b-roll and product; nobody selling to camera.
  • Avatar named: JOHN by default, SARAH for gifting seasons.
  • Feather or Santoku first; Blackout only as an iteration of a proven script.
  • Landing page: the 6 Reasons listicle, not the PDP.

Iterating a winner

  • Keep the hook and the body. Change the product, the editor, the market, the CTA or the voice.
  • Category Ite, Src-None, and the Ite- type in the name.

Naming

  • Name every ad with the v2.2 convention (skill nk-ad-naming or the web builder) so the next benchmark reads itself.
  • Web builder: video · static.

21Method and caveats

  • Source. All 642 briefs in the Notion Evergreen Video, Father's Day, EOFY and holiday databases, exported read-only in September 2026. Nothing was changed in Notion.
  • Tagging. Each brief was read in full (hook table, body table, persona and instruction callouts) and every field of the v2.2 convention was decided from the text, with the justifying quote stored next to each judgement call. Tags are in the repo under research/ad-naming-benchmark/lanes/.
  • Performance. Triple Whale, all 6 stores, January 2025 to 21 September 2026: spend, impressions, 3-second views, thruplays, clicks and conversion value by creative ID under Platform, Triple Attribution and First Click. Video length from the delivered file in Frame.io / creatives.
  • Readable. $1,000 of spend or more. 179 of the 405 launched ads. The other 463 briefs are named but carry no verdict.
  • Concentration. The spend-weighted averages are pulled by two families, What if (NK184, NK326, NK146) and Return (NK222, NK168, NK284, NK219, NK234). That is why every table shows the median and the win and loss rates, and why the verdicts in this document only call a trait solid when all three agree.
  • Correlation, not causation. Craftsmanship ads also tend to be long, serious, JOHN, Feather and voice-over. The recipe is the bundle; we have not tested the parts separately. The next step is to brief deliberate splits (same script, offer mid vs first; same script, face vs no face) and let the naming convention read the result.
  • Currency. Spend and revenue are as reported by Triple Whale per store; UK, AU and EU amounts are in local currency and blended, so absolute dollars are approximate. ROAS ratios are unaffected.
  • People fields. Strategist and editor are in the data but not in this document, because they inherit the same handful of ads and read as a ranking of people rather than of creative decisions.

22Reading a v2.2 name

Every token in the name is one of the tags used in this document, in a fixed order, separated by underscores. Example, our biggest winner:
